\sum_{\nu=1}^{\ell}\alpha_{\nu}p_{\iota(\nu)}\;=\;\sum_{\nu=1}^{\ell}\alpha_{\nu}p_{\iota^{*}(\nu)}\hskip 10.00002pt\Longleftrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\exists\tau\in\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{S}_{\ell_{i}}:\;\iota^{*}=\iota\circ\tau.

This forces
p_{\pm 1},\dots,p_{\pm n}\in A(\mathbb{C})
to be pairwise distinct: Indeed, assuming that we had

p_{i}=p_{j}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{for certain}\hskip 10.00002pti,j\in\{\pm 1,\dots,\pm n\}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}\hskip 10.00002pti\;\neq\;\pm j,

then the implication
\Longrightarrow
in the above equivalence would fail for any
\iota,\iota^{*}\in I(\ell,\pm n)
with

\iota(1)=i=-\iota^{*}(1),\hskip 10.00002pt\iota(2)=-j=-\iota^{*}(2)\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\iota(\nu)=\iota^{*}(\nu)\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{for $\nu=3,\dots,\ell$},

a contradiction. This shows that the
0
-cycle
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{v}
is reduced, hence

\mathbb{V}

must be reduced as well. For the complement of the big diagonal and antidiagonal we then get a bijection

I(\ell,\pm n)\;\longrightarrow\;(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{\times\ell}_{|U}\smallsetminus(\Delta_{\ell}\cup\Delta_{\ell}^{-}))_{v},\hskip 10.00002pt\iota\;\longmapsto\;(p_{\iota(1)},\dots,p_{\iota(\ell)},v)

and can conclude as in
lemma

5.18
that
\cc(P,\alpha)=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{\alpha}_{{\mathbb{S}}}
.
∎

5.7.
Weyl orbits for type
D

For
\tilde{G}=\Spin_{2n}
we again let
\varepsilon_{\pm 1},\dots,\varepsilon_{\pm n}\in\tilde{\mathsf{X}}
denote the weights of the standard representation of the orthogonal group, with the relations
\varepsilon_{-i}=-\varepsilon_{i}
. The fundamental weights are

\varpi_{i}\;=\;\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{i}&\textnormal{for $i<n-1$},\\
\tfrac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{n-1}-\varepsilon_{n})&\textnormal{for $i=n-1$},\\
\tfrac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{n-1}+\varepsilon_{n})&\textnormal{for $i=n$}.\\
\end{cases}

The first
n-2
fundamental weights are highest weights of wedge powers of the standard representation; the last two fundamental weights are the highest weights of the two spin representations. The dominant integral weights are the weights of the form

\alpha\;=\;\sum_{\nu=1}^{n}\alpha_{\nu}\varepsilon_{\nu}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}\hskip 10.00002pt\alpha_{1}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\alpha_{n-1}\geqslant|\alpha_{n}|\geqslant 0,

where
2\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
are either all even or all odd. Put
\ell=\max\{\nu\mid\alpha_{\nu}\neq 0\}
, and let
I_{\textup{even/odd}}(\ell,\pm n)
be the set of maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\colon\hskip-11.0pt}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1,\dots,\ell\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\pm 1,\dots,\pm n\}}

with the property that the map
\nu\mapsto|\iota(\nu)|
is still injective and the number of negative values of
\iota
is even resp. odd. Then
\alpha
has the Weyl group orbit

W.\alpha\;=\;\bigl\{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon_{\iota(1)}+\cdots+\alpha_{\ell}\varepsilon_{\iota(\ell)}\mid\iota\in I_{\textup{even/odd}}(\ell,\pm n)\bigr\}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}\hskip 10.00002pt\begin{cases}\text{even}&\text{if $\alpha_{n}\geqslant 0$},\\
\text{odd}&\text{if $\alpha_{n}<0$}.\end{cases}

Each weight in this orbit occurs for precisely
N(\alpha)
different choices of
\iota
, again the signs do not matter for this count.

In order to translate this back to geometry, we need to refine
definition

5.19
as follows:

Definition 5.21
.

Let
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}\in\mathcal{L}(A)
be a reduced clean cycle with
[-1]_{\ast}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}
, and let
U\subset\mathbb{P}_{A}
be an open dense subset over which all components of the cycle are finite and étale. Labelling the points in a general fiber of the Gauss map
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{|U}\to U
in pairs of opposite points as

\gamma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{972299}{219436}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{694499}{156740}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}}^{-1}(u)\;=\;\{p_{\pm 1},\dots,p_{\pm n}\}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}\hskip 10.00002ptp_{-i}\;=\;-p_{i},

we identify the monodromy group of the finite étale cover
\gamma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{972299}{219436}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{694499}{156740}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}|U}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{|U}\to U
as a subgroup of
(\pm 1)^{n}\rtimes\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. We say that the monodromy of the Gauss map is
even
if it is contained in the subgroup

(\pm 1)^{n}_{+}\rtimes\mathfrak{S}_{n}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{where}\hskip 10.00002pt(\pm 1)^{n}_{+}\;:=\;\{(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in(\pm 1)^{n}\mid a_{1}\cdots a_{n}=+1\}.

Then for
\ell=n
in
definition

5.19
we obtain that the cycle
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{[n]}_{\mathbb{S}}
on
A^{n}\times\mathbb{P}_{A}
splits as a sum

\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{[n]}_{\mathbb{S}}\;=\;\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{[n]}_{{\mathbb{S}},+}+\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{[n]}_{{\mathbb{S}},-}

where
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{[n]}_{{\mathbb{S}},\pm}\subset\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{[n]}_{\mathbb{S}}
are defined by the condition that their fiber over
u\in U(k)
contains an even resp. odd number of points with a negative sign, in other words

(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{[n]}_{{\mathbb{S}},\pm})_{u}\;:=\;\{(p_{i_{1}},\dots,p_{i_{n}},u)\mid\prod_{\nu=1}^{n}\mathrm{sgn}(i_{\nu})=\pm 1\}.

Note that this condition depends on the way we have labelled the points in the fiber
\gamma_{Z}^{-1}(u)
: The labelling by
\pm
has no intrinsic meaning and is only used as a notational device to separate the two pieces in the decomposition, none of the two pieces is distinguished. We define

\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{\alpha}_{{\mathbb{S}},\pm}\;:=\;\sigma_{\alpha\ast}(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{[n]}_{{\mathbb{S}},\pm})\;\in\;\mathcal{L}(A)

for
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and the sum morphism
\sigma_{\alpha}
as in
definition

5.19
. Note that while there is no intrinsic meaning to the labels
\pm
, it might nevertheless happen that
\pr_{A}(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{\alpha}_{{\mathbb{S}},-})\neq\pr_{A}(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{\alpha}_{{\mathbb{S}},+})
. We also note that by symmetry of
Z\subset A
we can assume without loss of generality that
\alpha_{n}\geqslant 0
.

Lemma 5.22
.

Let
G=G_{\omega}(P)
be semisimple with universal cover
\tilde{G}\simeq\Spin_{2n}
, and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{n}\subset\tilde{\mathsf{X}}
be a dominant weight of length
\ell
. If
\cc(P,\alpha)
is reduced, then

\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}:=\cc(P,\varepsilon_{1})

is reduced. In this case

\cc(P,\alpha)\;=\;\begin{cases}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{\alpha}_{\mathbb{S}}&\text{for $\ell<n$},\\
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{\alpha}_{{\mathbb{S}},\varepsilon}&\text{for $\ell=n$ and suitable $\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}$},\end{cases}

where on the right-hand side we identify
\alpha=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\varepsilon_{i}
with
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})
.

Proof.

Similar to the argument for type
B_{n}
.
∎

6.
Simplicity of the Tannaka group

We now take a closer look at the Tannaka group of the perverse intersection complex on a smooth nondivisible subvariety. By
corollary

5.15
, the corresponding representation is minuscule; the goal of this section is to show that, under suitable positivity assumptions, the Tannaka group is simple modulo its center.

6.1.
The simplicity criterion

For the rest of this section, we assume that
k
is algebraically closed of characteristic zero. Throughout, we fix a subvariety
X\subset A
and denote by

\omega\colon\hskip 10.00002pt\langle\delta_{X}\rangle\;\longrightarrow\;\Vect(\mathbb{F})

a fiber functor on the Tannaka category generated by the perverse intersection complex
\delta_{X}\in\textup{Perv}(A,\mathbb{F})
. Consider the reductive Tannaka group
G_{X,\omega}:=G_{\omega}(\delta_{X})
and denote by

G_{X,\omega}^{\ast}\;:=\;[G_{X,\omega}^{\circ},G_{X,\omega}^{\circ}]

the derived group of its connected component. This is a connected semisimple group, hence its Lie algebra is a product of simple Lie algebras. Recall that a connected algebraic group is
simple
if its Lie algebra is simple, or equivalently, it does not contain connected (reduced) normal subgroups. Writing
g:=\dim A
, the goal of this section is the following simplicity criterion:

Theorem 6.1
.

Suppose
g\geqslant 3
and let
X
be a smooth nondivisible subvariety with ample normal bundle. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

The algebraic group
G_{X,\omega}^{\ast}
is not simple;

(2)

There are smooth positive-dimensional subvarieties
X_{1},X_{2}\subset A
such that the sum morphism induces an isomorphism

X_{1}\times X_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}X.

The proof of this result will occupy the rest of this section, but let us first observe that the criterion applies in many cases. First, a smooth projective curve
X\subset A
generating
A
has ample normal bundle
[
Har71
, prop. 4.1]
, thus
G_{X,\omega}^{\ast}
is simple as soon as
X
is nondivisible and
g\geqslant 3
. More generally we have:

Corollary 6.2
.

Suppose
g\geqslant 3
and let
X\subset A
be a smooth nondivisible subvariety with ample normal bundle. Assume that one of the following conditions holds:

(1)

for
x\in X(k)
the image of the Albanese morphism
\alb_{X,x}\colon X\to\Alb(X)
is a nondegenerate subvariety of
\Alb(X)
in the sense of
section

2.3
, or

(2)

the natural map
\Alb(X)\to A
is an isogeny.

Then the algebraic group
G_{X,\omega}^{\ast}
is simple.

Proof of
corollary

6.2
.

For smooth connected subvarieties
X_{1},X_{2}\subset A
the image of all Albanese morphisms
X_{1}\times X_{2}\to\Alb(X_{1})\times\Alb(X_{2})
is degenerate provided that
0<\dim X_{1}+\dim X_{2}<2\dim A
. This shows the statement assuming (1). Now hypothesis (2) implies hypothesis (1): Indeed,
X\subset A
is nondegenerate by
theorem

2.8
and nondegeneracy is invariant under isogenies (
remark

2.6
).
∎

Remark 6.3
.

For a smooth integral subvariety
X\subset A
, condition (2) holds if the normal bundle of
X
is the direct sum of vector bundles
\mathcal{V}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}_{r}
with
r\geqslant 1
such that
\mathcal{V}_{i}
is
d_{i}
-ample
3
3

3

A line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on an integral variety
X
is
d
-ample
if there is an integer
n\geqslant 1
such that
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes n}
is globally generated and the fibers of the morphism
X\to\mathbb{P}(\textup{H}^{0}(X,\mathcal{L}^{\otimes n})^{\vee})
have dimension
\leqslant d
. Ordinary ampleness is equivalent to
0
-ampleness.
A vector bundle
\mathcal{E}
on
X
is
d
-ample
if the line bundle
\mathcal{O}(1)
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{\vee})
is
d
-ample.

and

\dim X>\max_{i=1,\dots,r}\rk\mathcal{V}_{i}+d_{i},

see
[
Deb95
, th. 4.5]
. By
theorem

2.8
the ampleness of the normal bundle of a smooth subvariety implies nondegeneracy, thus
theorem

6.1
applies to any smooth nondivisible subvariety
X\subset A
such that

•

the normal bundle of
X
is ample and
\dim X>g/2
, or

•

\dim X\geqslant 2
and
X
is a complete intersection of ample divisors in
A
.

6.2.
Product decomposition: from geometry to groups

We start by showing how to obtain a product decomposition of the Lie algebra of the Tannaka group starting from a product decomposition of the subvariety. Keeping the notation of
section

6.1
we write
G_{X,\omega}^{\ast}:=G_{\omega}^{\ast}(\delta_{X})
for any fixed fiber functor
\omega\colon\langle\delta_{X}\rangle\to\Vect(\mathbb{F})
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
G
be a simple simply connected algebraic group over
\mathbb{F}
and
V
,
W
nontrivial irreducible representations of
G
. Then
V\otimes W
is not minuscule.

Proof.

The minuscule representations of
G
are given up to isomorphism by the table on
section

1.4
. In particular, the highest weight of any minuscule representation is a fundamental weight. But the highest weight in
V\otimes W
is the sum of the highest weights of
V
and
W
, hence it is a sum of two dominant integral weights and therefore cannot be a fundamental weight.
∎

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
X_{1},X_{2}\subset A
be smooth subvarieties such that the sum morphism
\sigma\colon X_{1}\times X_{2}\to X:=X_{1}+X_{2}
is an isomorphism and
X
is nondivisible. Then we have an isomorphism of Lie algebras

\Lie G^{\ast}_{X,\omega}\simeq\Lie G^{\ast}_{X_{1},\omega}\oplus\Lie G^{\ast}_{X_{2},\omega}.

Proof.

Let
G
be the universal cover of the derived connected component of the Tannaka group of
\delta_{X_{1}}\oplus\delta_{X_{2}}
. The group
G
decomposes as a product

G=G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{n}

with
G_{1},\dots,G_{n}
simply connected simple (nontrivial) algebraic groups. Both perverse sheaves
\delta_{X_{1}}
and
\delta_{X_{2}}
belong to the tensor category
\langle\delta_{X_{1}}\oplus\delta_{X_{2}}\rangle
and therefore define representations of
G
. Since
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
are smooth and nondivisible (otherwise
X
would not be nondivisible), by
corollary

5.15
such representations are minuscule. By seeing them as representations of the product
G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{n}
they decompose as an external tensor product,

\omega(\delta_{X_{i}})=V_{i,1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes V_{i,n},\hskip 20.00003pti=1,2,

for representations
V_{i,\ell}
of
G_{\ell}
. Note that for all
i
,
\ell
the representation
V_{i,\ell}
is necessarily minuscule. By hypothesis the sum morphism
X_{1}\times X_{2}\to X
is an isomorphism, thus
\delta_{X}=\delta_{X_{1}}\ast\delta_{X_{2}}
by definition of the convolution product. In particular
\delta_{X}
belongs to
\langle\delta_{X_{1}}\oplus\delta_{X_{2}}\rangle
and as representations of
G
we have

\omega(\delta_{X})=\omega(\delta_{X_{1}})\otimes\omega(\delta_{X_{2}})=(V_{1,1}\otimes V_{2,1})\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes(V_{1,n}\otimes V_{2,n}).

Again by
corollary

5.15
, the representation
\omega(\delta_{X})
of
G
is minuscule because
X
is smooth and nondivisible. Thus the representation
V_{1,\ell}\otimes V_{2,\ell}
of
G_{\ell}
is also minuscule for each
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant n
. Now
lemma

6.4
implies that for
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant n
the representation
V_{i,\ell}
is trivial for exactly one
i\in\{1,2\}
. Strictly speaking
lemma

6.4
gives only the existence of such an
i
; however, if
V_{1,\ell}
and
V_{2,\ell}
were both trivial, then
G_{\ell}
would act trivially on
\omega(\delta_{X_{1}})\oplus\omega(\delta_{X_{2}})
contradicting the fact that
G_{\ell}
is a nontrivial simple factor of
G
. Resuming the proof, for
i=1,2
let
L_{i}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
be the subset made of those
\ell
for which
V_{i,\ell}
is nontrivial. Then
L_{1},L_{2}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
are complementary subsets and

\displaystyle G^{\ast}_{X_{i},\omega}

\displaystyle=\im(\textstyle\prod_{\ell\in L_{i}}G_{\ell}\to\GL(\omega(\delta_{X_{i}}))),\hskip 20.00003pti=1,2,

\displaystyle G^{\ast}_{X,\omega}

\displaystyle=\im(G\to\GL(\omega(\delta_{X}))).

Rather generally, for a simple simply connected algebraic group
H
and a nontrivial minuscule representation
W
of
H
, the kernel of
H\to\GL(W)
is finite. This gives isomorphisms of Lie algebras

\Lie G^{\ast}_{X,\omega}\simeq\Lie G\simeq\bigoplus_{\ell\in L_{1}}\Lie G_{\ell}\oplus\bigoplus_{\ell\in L_{2}}\Lie G_{\ell}\simeq\Lie G^{\ast}_{X_{1}}\oplus\Lie G^{\ast}_{X_{2}},

as desired.
∎

6.3.
Conic maps

In this section, we introduce the notion of conic map, which will turn out useful in dealing with conormal varieties (see
definition

6.7
). Recall that the
domain of definition
of a rational map is the maximal open subset of its source on which the map is well-defined.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
X
,
X^{\prime}\subset A
be integral subvarieties and
F\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\dashrightarrow\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X^{\prime}}
a rational map between their conormal varieties. Then there exists a unique rational map
f\colon X\dashrightarrow X^{\prime}
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}_{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Furthermore, the domain of definition of
f
contains the smooth locus
X^{\textup{reg}}\subset X
.

Proof.

Let
U
be the domain of definition of the rational map
F\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\dashrightarrow\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X^{\prime}}
. For any smooth point
x\in X^{\textup{reg}}(k)
the fiber
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X,x}
is a projective space. Every rational map from a projective space to an abelian variety is constant
[
Mil86
, cor. 3.9]
, so for
x\in X^{\textup{reg}}(k)
the morphism

\pr_{X^{\prime}}\circ\ F_{\rvert U_{x}}\colon\hskip 10.00002ptU_{x}\;:=\;U\cap\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X,x}\;\longrightarrow\;X^{\prime}\;\subset\;A

must be constant. Therefore, the morphism

\pr_{X^{\prime}}\circ\ F_{\rvert V}\colon\hskip 10.00002ptV\;:=\;U\cap\pr_{X}^{-1}(X^{\textup{reg}})\;\longrightarrow\;X^{\prime}\;\subset\;A

is constant along the fibers of the smooth morphism
\pr_{X}\colon V\to X^{\textup{reg}}
. Over the open subset
\pr_{X}(V)\subset X^{\textup{reg}}
the morphism
\pr_{X}
locally has sections, so we have

\pr_{X^{\prime}}\circ F=f\circ\pr_{X}

for a unique
f\colon\pr_{X}(V)\to X^{\prime}
. The latter extends to a morphism
f\colon X^{\textup{reg}}\to X^{\prime}
because a rational map from a variety to an abelian variety is defined at every smooth point of the source
[
Mil86
, th. 3.1]
.
∎

In the above proof, we have not used anything specific about conormal varieties. In fact, the only thing we used was that
\pr_{X}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\to X
is a projective bundle over
X^{\textup{reg}}\subset X
and that
X^{\prime}
embeds in an abelian variety. However, the conormal geometry will be taken into account by the following notion of a conic map:

Definition 6.7
.

In the setup of proposition
6.6
we call the rational map
f\colon X\dashrightarrow X^{\prime}
the
base
of
F\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\dashrightarrow\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X^{\prime}}
. We say that a rational map
F\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\dashrightarrow\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X^{\prime}}
is
conic
if the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}_{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{A}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{X^{\prime}}}

commutes, i.e., if
F
is compatible with the respective Gauss maps.

Example 6.8
.

Let
X
,
X^{\prime}\subset A
be integral subvarieties and
F\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\dashrightarrow\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X^{\prime}}
a conic map whose base is birational. Then

\deg\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\;=\;\deg\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X^{\prime}}

because the Gauss degree can be computed over any nonempty open subset of
\mathbb{P}_{A}
.

Note that even when the base
f\colon X\dashrightarrow X^{\prime}
of a conic map is defined everywhere, it is still not clear whether it is the restriction of an endomorphism of the abelian variety
A
. However, the results about conic maps in the rest of this section will suffice for our purpose:

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
X
,
X^{\prime}\subset A
be integral subvarieties and
F\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\dashrightarrow\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X^{\prime}}
a conic map. If the algebraic group
\Stab(X)
is finite, then the rational map
F
is dominant and generically finite.

Proof.

By
theorem

2.8
(1), the Gauss map
\gamma_{X}
is generically finite. It then follows from the commutative diagram in
definition

6.7
that the rational map
F
is also generically finite. Since
\dim(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X})=\dim(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X^{\prime}})=g-1=\dim(\mathbb{P}_{A})
, it follows that
F
is also dominant, being a generically finite map between varieties of the same dimension.
∎

6.4.
Product decompositions: from groups to geometry

We now explain how to obtain from a product decomposition for the Lie algebra of the Tannaka group a product decomposition for conormal varieties, using the above results about conic maps. Borrowing notation from
section

6.1
we write
G_{X,\omega}^{\ast}:=G_{\omega}^{\ast}(\delta_{X})
for any fixed fiber functor
\omega\colon\langle\delta_{X}\rangle\to\Vect(\mathbb{F})
.

Proposition 6.10
.

Assume
X\subset A
is a smooth nondivisible subvariety and
G_{X,\omega}^{\ast}
is not simple. Then for
i=1,2
there are integral subvarieties
X_{i}\subset A
with
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{i}}\in\langle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\rangle
of Gauss degree
\deg(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{i}})>1
, conic maps
F_{i}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\dashrightarrow\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{i}}
and integer
n\geqslant 1
with the following properties:

(1)

We have an identity of cycles
[n]_{\ast}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{[n](X)}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{1}}\circ\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{2}}
;

(2)

The following square is commutative

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}_{[n](X)}}

\mathbb{V}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}_{X_{1}}\circ\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}\times F_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[n]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

where

\mathbb{V}

and
\sigma
are as in
definition

5.2
;

(3)

The rational maps
F_{i}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\dashrightarrow\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{i}}
are dominant and generically finite;

(4)

The base of
F_{i}
is a morphism
f_{i}\colon X\to X_{i}
which is surjective.

Proof.

By
corollary

4.4
and
lemma

A.1
we may assume
k=\mathbb{C}
, which will allow us later to use the results about characteristic cycles in
theorem

5.11
.
By
corollary

3.5
the group
G_{X,\omega}^{\ast}
does not change if we replace
X
by
X+a
for any
a\in A(k)
, and it clearly suffices to achieve properties (1)-(4) for any such translate. We will therefore assume

\det(\delta_{X})\;=\;\delta_{0}

so that the connected component
G:=G_{X,\omega}^{\circ}
is semisimple by
proposition

3.3
. If the group
G_{X,\omega}^{\ast}
is not simple modulo its center, then by the structure theory of semisimple groups there are simply connected semisimple groups
G_{1},G_{2}\not\simeq\{1\}
and an isogeny

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\colon\hskip-11.0pt}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{G}\;:=\;G_{1}\times G_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

Then
V:=\omega(\delta_{X})
restricts to an irreducible representation of the covering group
\tilde{G}
and as such it decomposes as

V_{\rvert\tilde{G}}\;\simeq\;V_{1}\otimes V_{2}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with irreducible}\hskip 10.00002ptV_{i}\;\in\;\textup{Rep}_{\mathbb{F}}(G_{i})\;\subset\;\textup{Rep}_{\mathbb{F}}(\tilde{G}).

Note that both factors
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
must act nontrivially on
V
since otherwise they would not appear in the Tannaka group. In particular, we have
\dim V_{i}\geqslant 2
as any one-dimensional representation of a connected semisimple group is trivial.

Let
n=m\cdot\deg(p)
for the smallest integer
m\geqslant 1
with
m\cdot\Gamma_{\cc(\delta_{X})}=\{0\}
. Since
X
is smooth, its perverse intersection complex has characteristic cycle
\cc(\delta_{X})=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}
. Via the first part of
theorem

5.11
, the above decomposition as a tensor product of two representations of the universal covering group yields clean cycles
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{1},\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{2}\in\langle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\rangle
such that

[n]_{\ast}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{1}\circ\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{2},

and the second part of the theorem implies that
\deg\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{i}=\dim V_{i}\geqslant 2
.
Moreover, by
lemma

5.14
the cycles
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{i}
are effective. Since we assumed the subvariety
X\subset A
to be nondivisible, the morphism
[n]\colon X\to{[n](X)}
is dominant and birational, in fact we have

[n]_{\ast}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{[n](X)}

as an identity of cycles by
lemma

2.4
.
Altogether, it follows that

\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{[n](X)}\;=\;[n]_{*}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\;=\;\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{1}\circ\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{2}.

The cycle on the left-hand side is reduced and irreducible, so the same must hold for both factors on the right-hand side because the convolution product
\circ
on cycles is bilinear and the convolution of any two clean effective cycles is again a clean effective cycle. Hence, there exist integral subvarieties
X_{i}\subset A
with
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{i}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{i}}
. By definition of
\circ
, we have

\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{1}\circ\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{2}\;=\;\sigma_{*}(\overline{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{1}\mid U}\times_{U}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{2}\mid U}})

where
\sigma\colon A\times A\times\mathbb{P}_{A}\to A\times\mathbb{P}_{A}
denotes the sum morphism and
U\subset\mathbb{P}_{A}
is as in
definition

5.2
. The multiplicities of the cycle-theoretic pushforward on the right-hand side are given by the degree of the sum morphism

\sigma\colon\hskip 10.00002pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}\;:=\;\overline{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{1}\mid U}\times_{U}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{2}\mid U}}\;\longrightarrow\;\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{[n](X)}

on the various components of its source. Since the cycle
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{1}\circ\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{2}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{[n](X)}
is integral as observed above, it follows in fact that the fiber product

\mathbb{V}

is integral and is mapped birationally onto its image by
\sigma
.
Consider then the composition of rational maps

F_{i}\colon\hbox to627.86pt{\vbox to22.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 313.92673pt\lower-8.90973pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

where
\pr_{i}
denotes be the projection onto the
i
-th factor.
By construction,
F_{i}
is a conic map, and by
proposition

6.6
its base
f_{i}\colon X\dashrightarrow X_{i}
is defined on all of
X
because we assumed
X
to be smooth. Moreover, by
proposition

6.9
the rational map
F_{i}
is dominant and generically finite, so the morphism
f_{i}
is surjective.
∎

6.5.
Proof of
theorem

6.1

We can now prove the simplicity criterion for Tannaka groups as follows. Thanks to
proposition

6.5
only the implication (1)
\implies
(2) is left to be shown. Suppose that the algebraic group
G_{X,\omega}^{\ast}
is not simple. According to
[
LS20
, Lemma 4.6]
, this is never the case when
X
is a smooth ample divisor, thus from now we may assume
\dim X<g-1
. Let
n
,
X_{i}
,
F_{i}
and
f_{i}\colon X\to X_{i}
be as in
proposition

6.10
.
For
i=1,2
, the Gauss map of
X_{i}
is a finite morphism because the one of
X
is by
theorem

2.8
and
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{i}}\in\langle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\rangle
by construction. It is therefore possible to apply
corollary

5.7
and deduce the equality

\dim X=\dim X_{1}+\dim X_{2}

from the identity of cycles
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{1}}\circ\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{2}}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{[n](X)}
and the hypothesis
\dim X<g-1
. Moreover, the subvarieties
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are nondegenerate by
theorem

2.8
. Thus,
lemma

2.7
implies that the sum

X_{1}+X_{2}\;\subset\;A

is a nondegenerate subvariety in
A
and the sum morphism
\sigma\colon X_{1}\times X_{2}\to X_{1}+X_{2}
is generically finite. Therefore, the conormal cone
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{1}+X_{2}}
appears as a summand with multiplicity
\deg(\sigma)
in the cycle
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{1}}\circ\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{2}}
. But we know that

\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{1}}\circ\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X_{2}}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{[n](X)}
, hence

\deg(\sigma)\;=\;1\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 10.00002ptX_{1}+X_{2}\;=\;[n](X).

Together with
proposition

6.10
(2) this gives the following commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[n\right](X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}\times X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}+X_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}\times f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[n]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

The nondivisibility assumption on
X
implies that the morphism
[n]\colon X\to[n](X)
is finite birational. This forces
f=(f_{1}\times f_{2})
to be finite birational. As
X
is smooth, this says that
f
is the normalization morphism. For
i=1,2
let
\tilde{X}_{i}
be the normalization of
X_{i}
. The morphism
\tilde{f}\colon X\to\tilde{X}_{1}\times\tilde{X}_{2}
induced by
f
is an isomorphism, thus
\tilde{X}_{1}
and
\tilde{X}_{2}
are smooth. Identifying
X
with
\tilde{X}_{1}\times\tilde{X}_{2}
permits to embed
\tilde{X}_{1}
and
\tilde{X}_{2}
in
A
and to write
\Alb(X)=\Alb(\tilde{X}_{1})\times\Alb(\tilde{X_{2}})
. By suitably embedding
X
,
\tilde{X}_{1}
, and
\tilde{X}_{2}
in their Albanese variety, we have the following identity:

(\tilde{X}_{1}\times\{0\})+(\{0\}\times\tilde{X}_{2})=X\subset\Alb(X).

The commutativity of the following square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Alb(\tilde{X}_{1})\times\{0\})\times(\{0\}\times\Alb(\tilde{X}_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Alb(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

where the horizontal arrows are the sum morphisms and the vertical ones are given by universal property of the Albanese, implies the desired equality
X=\tilde{X}_{1}+\tilde{X}_{2}
.
∎

7.
Wedge powers

We now characterize low-dimensional smooth subvarieties whose Tannaka group is the image of a special linear group acting on a nontrivial wedge power of its standard representation. In the case of hypersurfaces, Lawrence and Sawin show that such groups do not occur
[
LS20
, lemma 4.10]
using combinatorial properties of Eulerian numbers. In higher codimension, wedge powers do occur, but we show by geometric arguments that they only arise from symmetric powers of curves.

7.1.
Statement of the main result

As in the previous section, we assume the field
k
to be algebraically closed of characteristic zero. Fix a subvariety
X\subset A
and a fiber functor

\omega\colon\langle\delta_{X}\rangle\to\Vect(\mathbb{F})
on the Tannaka category generated by the perverse intersection complex of
X
. As in
section

6.1
, put
G_{X,\omega}:=G_{\omega}(\delta_{X})
and denote by

G_{X,\omega}^{\ast}\;:=\;[G^{\circ}_{X,\omega},G^{\circ}_{X,\omega}]

the derived group of its connected component of the identity. We are interested in the following situation:

Definition 7.1
.

Let
r\geqslant 1
be an integer. We say
X\subset A
is an
r
-th wedge power
if we have

G_{X,\omega}^{\ast}\simeq\Alt^{r}(\SL_{n}(\mathbb{F}))\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with the standard action on}\hskip 10.00002pt\omega(\delta_{X})\simeq\Alt^{r}(\mathbb{F}^{n})

for some
n\geqslant 1
. Notice that if
r
is given, then
n
is determined by the topological Euler characteristic

\chi(\delta_{X})\;=\;\dim_{\mathbb{F}}(\omega(\delta_{X}))\;=\;\tbinom{n}{r}.

If
X
is an
r
-th wedge power, then by duality it is also an
(n-r)
-th wedge power.

The typical example of wedge powers arises from symmetric powers of curves, as announced in the introduction:

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
C\subset A
be a smooth projective curve and
r\geqslant 2
an integer. If the sum morphism

s\colon\hskip 10.00002pt\Sym^{r}C\;\longrightarrow\;X\;=\;C+\cdots+C\;\subset\;A

is an isomorphism onto its image, then this image
X\subset A
