is an
r
-th wedge power.

Proof.

Replacing
A
by the abelian subvariety
\langle C\rangle\subset A
, we may assume that
C
generates
A
. Fix a point
p\in C(k)
. The morphism
\alb_{C,p}\colon C\hookrightarrow\Alb(C)
induces a sum morphism

\Sym^{r}(C)\;\longrightarrow\;\Alb(C).

Let
W_{r}\subset\Alb(C)
denote the image of this sum morphism. The embedding
C\hookrightarrow A
also induces a morphism
\Alb(C)\to A
; this last morphism is surjective because we assumed
A=\langle C\rangle
.
We obtain the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{r}(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Alb(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{r}(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

The square on the left implies that the morphism
W_{r}\to X
is an isomorphism, therefore
W_{r}
is smooth (hence by the Riemann singularity theorem the curve
C
is not
r
-gonal, so in particular it is not hyperelliptic if
r\geqslant 2
).

Fixing any
r-2\geqslant 0
points on the curve and varying the remaining two points, one obtains from the above also that the morphisms
\Sym^{2}(C)\to W_{2}\subset\Alb(C)
and
\Sym^{2}(C)\to C+C\subset A
have the same fibers. So we can apply the variant of Larsen’s alternative in
[
KW15b
, section 6]
to the perverse sheaf
\delta_{C}\in\textup{Perv}(A,\mathbb{F})
to see that

G^{\ast}_{\omega}(\delta_{C})\simeq\SL_{n}(\mathbb{F})\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with the standard action on}\hskip 10.00002pt\omega(\delta_{C})\simeq\mathbb{F}^{n}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{for}\hskip 10.00002ptn=\chi(\delta_{C}).

The above diagram then shows

G_{X,\omega}^{\ast}\simeq\Alt^{r}(\SL_{n}(\mathbb{F}))

and

\omega(\delta_{X})\simeq\Alt^{r}(\mathbb{F}^{n})
.
∎

The goal of this section is to show a converse to the above lemma. More precisely, we obtain the following complete classification of wedge powers for all nondivisible smooth subvarieties
X\subset A
of high codimension whose Gauss map
\gamma_{X}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\to\mathbb{P}_{A}
is finite:

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
X\subset A
be a nondivisible smooth subvariety with ample normal bundle, and suppose that its Euler characteristic is
\chi(\delta_{X})=\tbinom{n}{r}
for some integer
r
with
1<r\leqslant n/2
. If
2\dim X<\dim A-1
, then the following are equivalent:

(1)

The subvariety
X\subset A
is an
r
-th wedge power.

(2)

There is a nondegenerate irreducible smooth projective curve
C\subset A
such that

•

X=C+\cdots+C\subset A
is the sum of
r
copies of
C
, and

•

the sum morphism
\Sym^{r}C\to X
is an isomorphism.

In view of
lemma

7.2
we only need to show the implication
(1)\Longrightarrow(2)
, which will take up the rest of this section.

7.2.
Structure of the proof

The proof relies on three independent steps. The first step is to show that the structure of wedge powers is reflected by characteristic cycles: To any subvariety
Z\subset A
and an integer
r\geqslant 1
we will attach a clean effective cycle

\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}\;\in\;\mathcal{L}(A),

and we show:

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
X\subset A
be a smooth nondivisible subvariety with
\dim X>0
that is an
r
-th wedge power for some integer
r\geqslant 1
. Then there is an integral subvariety
Z\subset A
with
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}\in\langle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\rangle
such that

\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}\;=\;\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{[e](X)}

for some integer
e\geqslant 1
. The Gauss degrees are related by

\deg\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}=\tbinom{n}{r}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{where}\hskip 10.00002ptn=\deg\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z},

and if the Gauss map
\gamma_{X}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\to\mathbb{P}_{A}
is a finite morphism, then so is
\gamma_{Z}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}\to\mathbb{P}_{A}
.

For the construction of the clean cycle
\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}
and the proof of the above result, see
section

7.3
. Once we have this, the second step in our classification of wedge powers will be to prove a monotonicity statement for the cycles
\Alt^{r}(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z})
as a function of
r
. Let

\Alt^{r}Z\;:=\;\im\Bigl(\pr_{A}\colon\Supp(\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z})\to A\Bigr)\;\subset\;A

be the image of the support of the clean effective cycle
\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}\in\mathcal{L}(A)
under the projection to the abelian variety, then we will show:

Theorem 7.5
.

Let
Z\subset A
be an integral subvariety whose Gauss map is a finite morphism of degree
n=\deg(\gamma_{Z})
. Suppose there exists an integer
r\geqslant 1
with
r\leqslant n/2
such that

\dim\Alt^{r}Z<(\dim A-1)/2.

Then we have

r\dim Z<\dim A.

The proof of this will be given in
section

7.4
. Finally, the last step for our classification of wedge powers will be to show that in the given dimension range, the smoothness of
\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}
forces
Z
to be a curve. More precisely:

Theorem 7.6
.

Let
X,Z\subset A
be integral subvarieties whose Gauss maps are finite morphisms. Suppose moreover that
X
is smooth and nondivisible and that there are integers
e,r\geqslant 1
such that

\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{[e](X)}.

If
r\dim Z<\dim A
, then
Z
is a curve and

(1)

the normalization
C
of
Z
embeds in
A
,

(2)

X=C+\cdots+C\subset A
is the sum of
r
copies of
C
, embedded suitably in
A
,

(3)

the sum morphism
\Sym^{r}C\to X
is an isomorphism.

We will prove this in
section

7.5
. Before coming to the details, let us note how the above three results conclude the classification of wedge powers:

Proof of
theorem

7.3
.

Suppose that
X\subset A
satisfies the assumptions of the theorem and that it is an
r
-th wedge power for some
r\in\mathbb{N}
with
1<r\leqslant n/2
where
\deg\gamma_{X}=\tbinom{n}{r}
. By
theorem

7.4
then

\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{[e](X)}.

for some integer
e\geqslant 1
and some integral subvariety
Z\subset A
.
Since
[e]\colon A\to A
is an isogeny, we have

\dim[e](X)=\dim X<(\dim A-1)/2

by our dimension assumption on
X
.
Theorem

7.5
then shows

r\dim Z<\dim A,

and hence
theorem

7.6
gives the desired result.
∎

7.3.
Characteristic cycles of wedge powers

We now explain how to compute characteristic cycles of wedge powers.
Let
Z\subset A
be a subvariety with dominant Gauss map
\gamma_{Z}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}\to\mathbb{P}_{A}
. For an integer
r\geqslant 1
we consider as in
definition

5.16
the Zariski closure

\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}^{[r]}\;:=\;\overline{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{\times r}_{Z\mid U}\smallsetminus\Delta_{r}}\;\subset\;A^{r}\times\mathbb{P}_{A}

where
U\subset\mathbb{P}_{A}
is any open dense subset over which the Gauss map
\gamma_{Z}
is finite and flat.
Let
\sigma\colon A^{r}\times\mathbb{P}_{A}\to A\times\mathbb{P}_{A},(z_{1},\dots,z_{r},\xi)\mapsto(z_{1}+\cdots+z_{r},\xi)
be the sum morphism and put

\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}\;:=\;\tfrac{1}{r!}\,\sigma_{*}(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}^{[r]})\;\in\;\mathcal{L}(A),

which is the special case
\alpha=(1,\dots,1)=(1^{r})
of the cycle in
definition

5.16
.

Proof of
theorem

7.4
.

By
corollary

4.4
and
lemma

A.1
we may assume that
k=\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
so that we can use the results about characteristic cycles from
section

5
. Replacing
X
by a translate we may assume by
corollary

3.5
that the connected component of the group
G_{\omega}(\delta_{X})
is semisimple. Its universal cover is then isomorphic to
\tilde{G}\simeq\SL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
for some
n\geqslant 1
, so the setup in
section

5.5
applies to
P=\delta_{X}
. By assumption

\omega(P)\;\simeq\;\Alt^{r}(\mathbb{C}^{n}).

Since
\dim\omega(P)>1
for any non-negligible perverse sheaf
P
which is not a skyscraper sheaf
[
Wei15b
]
, we have
1<r<n
. The highest weight of the above wedge power representation of
\SL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is the fundamental weight
\alpha=\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{r}
. With notation as in
definition

5.12
, the Weyl group orbit
W.\alpha
consists precisely of the weights in the representation
\xi(P^{\circ})
, so
\xi^{-1}[W.\alpha]=[P^{\circ}]
in the Grothendieck ring
K(\langle P^{\circ}\rangle)
. It follows that

\displaystyle\cc(P,\alpha)

\displaystyle\;=\;\cc(\xi^{-1}[W.d\alpha])

with notation as in
definition

5.12

\displaystyle\;=\;[d]_{*}\cc(P^{\circ})

since
\xi^{-1}[W.\alpha]=[P^{\circ}]

\displaystyle\;=\;[e]_{*}\cc(P)

for
e:=dm
and
P^{\circ}:=[m]_{\ast}P

\displaystyle\;=\;[e]_{*}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}

since
\cc(\delta_{X})=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}
for smooth
X

\displaystyle\;=\;\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{[e](X)}

by
lemma

2.4
, since
X
is nondivisible.

In particular, the cycle
\cc(P,\alpha)
is integral. So by
lemma

5.18
the cycle
\cc(P,\varepsilon_{1})
is integral as well, hence of the form
\cc(P,\varepsilon_{1})=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}
for an integral subvariety
Z\subset A
, and we have

\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{[e](X)}\;=\;\cc(P,\alpha)\;=\;\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}^{\alpha}\;=\;\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}.

Then
\deg\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}=\deg\cc(P,\alpha)=\dim\omega(P)=\tbinom{n}{r}
. For the statement about the finiteness of the Gauss map, recall from
remark

5.13
that the cycle
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}=\cc(P,\varepsilon_{1})
lies in the subring
\langle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}\rangle\subset\mathcal{L}(A)
. As such, it appears in some convolution power of the cycle
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{X}
. Recalling the definition of the convolution product, this implies that if the Gauss map for
\gamma_{X}
is finite, then so is the one for
\gamma_{Z}
.
∎

Remark 7.7
.

The discussion in the proof of
theorem

7.4
shows that for any reduced subvariety
Z\subset A
whose Gauss map is dominant of degree
n=\deg(\gamma_{Z})
and any integer
r\geqslant 1
we have:

•

The projection
\gamma_{Z,r}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}^{[r]}\to\mathbb{P}_{A}
is dominant (and hence generically finite) if and only if
r\leqslant n
. In that case

\deg\gamma_{Z,r}\;=\;r!\tbinom{n}{r}.

•

If
\gamma_{Z}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}\to\mathbb{P}_{A}
is a finite morphism, then so is
\gamma_{Z,r}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}^{[r]}\to\mathbb{P}_{A}
.

7.4.
Dimension estimates for wedge powers

We will deduce
theorem

7.5
from a monotonicity property of wedge powers. To formulate this, recall that for
Z\subset A
and an integer
i\geqslant 1
we put

\Alt^{i}Z:=\pr_{A}(\Supp(\Alt^{i}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}))\subset A
.
We are interested in its dimension

d_{Z}(i)\;:=\;\dim\Alt^{i}Z.

Example 7.8
.

Let
Z
be a smooth projective curve of genus
g>1
, embedded via a suitable translate of the Abel-Jacobi map in its Jacobian variety
A=\Alb(Z)
. Then by
[
Krä20
, examples 3.1 and 4.1]
,

d_{Z}(i)\;=\;\begin{cases}i&\textnormal{for $1\leqslant i\leqslant g-1$},\\
g-1-i&\textnormal{for $g-1\leqslant i\leqslant 2g-2$}.\end{cases}

Here
d_{Z}
is not monotonous, but it is so between zero and
g-1=\deg\gamma_{Z}/2
.

The following monotonicity result shows that the behavior in the above example is typical for wedge powers in small degrees:

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
Z\subset A
be an integral nondegenerate subvariety, and let
r\geqslant 1
be an integer such that
r\dim Z<\dim A
.

(1)

Let
Y:=Z+\cdots+Z\subset A
be the sum of
r
copies of
Z
. Then the sum morphism

\sigma\colon\hskip 10.00002pt\Sym^{r}Z\;=\;Z^{r}/\mathfrak{S}_{r}\;\longrightarrow\;Y

is generically finite, and the subvariety
Y\subset A
is nondegenerate.

(2)

The clean cycle
\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}
contains
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Y}
with multiplicity
d=\deg(\sigma)\geqslant 1
, and we have

\Alt^{r}Z\;=\;Y.

(3)

For general
z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{r})\in Z^{r}(k)
, the fiber of
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Y}
over the point
y=\sigma(z)
is

\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Y,y}\;=\;\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z,z_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2032337}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1662625}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1091722}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z,z_{r}}\;\subset\;\mathbb{P}_{A}.

Proof.

The image
Y=Z+\cdots+Z
of the sum morphism
\sigma\colon\Sym^{r}Z\to Y
is a proper subvariety of
A
because
\dim Y\leqslant\dim\Sym^{r}Z=r\dim Z<\dim A
. As we assumed
Z\subset A
to be nondegenerate, it then follows by
lemma

2.7
that the morphism
\sigma
is generically finite and that
Y\subset A
is again nondegenerate.

This last property implies that the Gauss map
\gamma_{Y}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Y}\to\mathbb{P}_{A}
is generically finite and dominant, see
theorem

2.8
. Let
W\subset Y^{\textup{reg}}
be a nonempty open subset such that
V:=\sigma^{-1}(W)
is contained in
(Z^{\textup{reg}})^{r}\smallsetminus\Delta_{r}
and the sum morphism
\sigma\colon V\to W
is finite étale. If we view the tangent spaces as subspaces of
\Lie(A)
, then for every point
z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{r})\in V(k)
and
y=\sigma(z)
the tangent map

T_{z}(\sigma)\colon\hskip 10.00002ptT_{z_{1}}(Z)\times\cdots\times T_{z_{r}}(Z)\;\longrightarrow\;T_{y}(Y)

is the restriction of the sum map
\Lie(A)^{r}\to\Lie(A)
. In particular,
T_{z_{i}}(Z)\subset T_{y}(Y)
and hence
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Y,y}\subset\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z,z_{i}}
for all
i
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{P}_{A}
be a nonempty open subset over which the Gauss maps
\gamma_{Y}
and
\gamma_{Z}
are finite étale, and such that
\pr_{Y}(\gamma_{Y}^{-1}(U))\subset W
. For any
\xi\in U(k)
and
z\in V(k)
with
\sigma(z)\in\gamma_{Y}^{-1}(\xi)
, we have

(z_{1},\dots,z_{r},\xi)\;\in\;\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z|U}^{\times r}\smallsetminus\Delta_{r}\;\subset\;\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}^{[r]}

So the image of
\pr_{Z,r}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}^{[r]}\to Z^{r}
contains an open dense subset of
Z^{r}
. Hence by properness, the morphism
\pr_{Z,r}
is surjective and then
\Alt^{r}Z=Y
by definition. In particular,
\dim\Alt^{r}Z=\dim Y=r\dim Z
, where the last equality follows from the generic finiteness of the sum morphism
\sigma\colon\Sym^{r}Z\to Y
.
The statement about the general fiber of
\pr_{Y}\colon\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}\to Y
follows from the fact that for general
(z_{1},\dots,z_{r})
we have

T_{y}(Y)\;=\;T_{z_{1}}(Z)\oplus\cdots\oplus T_{z_{r}}(Z)

because the summands on the right-hand side span
T_{y}(Y)
and their dimension adds up to
r\dim Z=\dim Y=\dim T_{y}(Y)
; passing to the corresponding normal spaces gives
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Y,y}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z,z_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z,z_{r}}
and the claim follows since
\Supp(\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z})_{y}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Y,y}
for
y\in Y(k)
general. Finally, it is also clear from the above discussion that the clean cycle
\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}
contains the component
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Y}
with multiplicity
d=\deg(\sigma)
.
∎

Corollary 7.10
.

Let
Z\subset A
be an integral nondegenerate subvariety of positive dimension. Then

\deg(\gamma_{Z})\;\geqslant\;2\frac{\dim A}{\dim Z}-2.

Proof.

Proposition

7.9
shows that the map
r\mapsto d_{Z}(r):=\dim\Alt^{r}Z
is strictly increasing on the interval
\{0,1,\dots,r_{0}\}
, where
r_{0}=\max\{r\mid r\dim Z<\dim A\}
. On the other hand, we have

\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}\;=\;\Alt^{n-r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{p-Z}

where
n=\deg(\gamma_{Z})
and where the point
p\in A(k)
is defined by
\Alt^{n}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{\{p\}}
. It follows that

\dim\Alt^{r}Z\;=\;\dim\Alt^{n-r}Z.

The left-hand side is strictly increasing for
r\in\{0,1,\dots,r_{0}\}
, the right-hand side is strictly decreasing for
r\in\{n-r_{0},\dots,n\}
.
This forces
r_{0}\leqslant n-r_{0}
, hence
n\geqslant 2r_{0}
. Then
(r_{0}+1)\dim Z\geqslant\dim A
implies

(n/2+1)\cdot\dim Z\geqslant\dim A

as desired.
∎

For smooth subvarieties
Z\subset A
, the degree of the Gauss map
\gamma_{Z}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}\to\mathbb{P}_{A}
is equal to the topological Euler characteristic of
\delta_{Z}
, see
section

5.3
. So for smooth curves
Z\subset A
with
\langle Z\rangle=A
,
corollary

7.10
says that the curve has genus
\geqslant\dim A
; this follows of course also directly from the fact that for such curves the morphism
\Alb(Z)\to A
must be surjective. Note that here the bound in
corollary

7.10
is sharp.

Proposition

7.9
gives a monotonicity statement for wedge powers, but in order to apply it we need to have an a priori bound on
\dim Z
. The following result will allow us to start instead from a bound only on some wedge power
\dim\Alt^{r}Z
, since the bound will be inherited by all lower wedge powers:

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
Z\subset A
be a reduced subvariety whose Gauss map is a finite morphism of degree
n
. Let
r
be an integer with
1\leqslant r\leqslant n/2
, and assume that

\dim\Alt^{r}Z<(\dim A-1)/2.

Then the function
d_{Z}\colon\{1,\dots,r\}\to\mathbb{N},i\mapsto d_{Z}(i):=\dim\Alt^{i}Z
is nondecreasing, in particular

\dim\Alt^{i}Z<(\dim A-1)/2\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{for all}\hskip 10.00002pti\in\{1,\dots,r\}.

Proof.

We only need to show
d_{Z}(r-1)\leqslant d_{Z}(r)
, because we can then proceed by descending induction. Put

\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{i}\;:=\;(\Alt^{i}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z})\circ(\Alt^{i}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{-Z})\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{for}\hskip 10.00002pti\;=\;1,\dots,n.

The support of this cycle is the closure of the subset of points in
(A\times\mathbb{P}_{A})(k)
of the form

(x_{1}+\cdots+x_{i}-y_{1}-\cdots-y_{i},\xi)

where

•

\xi\in\mathbb{P}_{A}(k)
is a cotangent direction such that
\pr_{A}(\gamma_{Z}^{-1}(\xi))\subset Z^{\textup{reg}}(k)
,

•

\{x_{1},\dots,x_{i}\},\{y_{1},\dots,y_{i}\}\subset\pr_{A}(\gamma_{Z}^{-1}(\xi))
are subsets of cardinality
i
.

For general
\xi\in\mathbb{P}_{A}(k)
the fiber
\gamma_{Z}^{-1}(\xi)
consists of
n\geqslant 2r>2(r-1)+1
distinct points, hence for any two subsets
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{r-1}\}
and
\{y_{1},\dots,y_{r-1}\}
as above there is a point
p
in
\pr_{A}(\gamma_{Z}^{-1}(\xi))
which belongs to neither of the two subsets. By writing the point

z\;:=\;x_{1}+\cdots+x_{r-1}-y_{1}-\cdots-y_{r-1}

as
z+p-p
, we find that the point
(z,\xi)
lies in the support of
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{r}
. Varying
\xi
and the chosen subsets of points in the fiber of the Gauss map, we obtain the inclusion

\Supp(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{r-1})\subset\Supp(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{r})

for the supports. Since both cycles are linear combinations of conormal varieties and hence pure of the same dimension
\dim A-1
, it follows that we have

(7.1)

\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{r}=c\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{r-1}+E

where
c>0
is a rational number and
E
is an effective cycle with rational coefficients.

The finiteness of the Gauss map
\gamma_{Z}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}\to\mathbb{P}_{A}
implies that
\Alt^{i}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}\to\mathbb{P}_{A}
is finite as well. Indeed
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{[i]}_{Z}
is by definition a subvariety inside the
i
-fold fiber product
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}\times_{\mathbb{P}_{A}}\cdots\times_{\mathbb{P}_{A}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}
and the fiber product of finite morphisms is a finite morphism. By design the sum map
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}^{[i]}_{Z}\to\Supp(\Alt^{i}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z})
is surjective and compatible with Gauss maps, implying the desired finiteness. By
lemma

5.5
, the finiteness of the Gauss map for
\Alt^{i}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}
allows to compute the total Segre class of
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{i}=\Alt^{i}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}\circ\Alt^{i}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{-Z}
as a Pontryagin product

s(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{i})\;=\;s(\Alt^{i}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z})\ast s(\Alt^{i}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{-Z})\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{in}\hskip 10.00002pt\CH_{<g}(A)\;:=\;\CH_{\bullet}(A)/\CH_{g}(A).

Comparing this with (
7.1
), we obtain in the truncated Chow ring
\CH_{<g}(A)
an identity

s(\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z})\ast s(\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{-Z})\;=\;cs(\Alt^{r-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z})\ast s(\Alt^{r-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{-Z})+\cdots

where
\cdots
stands for effective cycle classes (possibly zero).
The left-hand side of this equality vanishes in all degrees
>2d_{Z}(r)
since
s_{i}(\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z})=s_{i}(\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{-Z})=0
for all
i>d_{Z}(r)
. On the other hand,
lemma

5.6
says that on the right-hand side the Pontryagin product

s(\Alt^{r-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z})\ast s(\Alt^{r-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{-Z})

is nonzero and effective in all degrees
\leqslant\min\{2d_{Z}(r-1),\dim A-1\}
because the subvariety
\Alt^{r-1}Z
has finite Gauss map and hence all its irreducible components are nondegenerate by
theorem

2.8
. So a comparison of the left and right-hand side yields

\min\{2d_{Z}(r-1),\dim A-1\}\leqslant 2d_{Z}(r)<\dim A-1,

whence
2d_{Z}(r-1)\leqslant\dim A-1
and
d_{Z}(r-1)\leqslant d_{Z}(r)
.
∎

Proof of
theorem

7.5
.

Let
g:=\dim A
.
We argue by contradiction: If
r\dim Z\geqslant g
, consider the integer

s\;:=\;\max\{i\in\mathbb{N}\mid i\dim Z<g\}\;<\;r.

On the one hand, we have

\displaystyle s\dim Z

\displaystyle\;=\;\dim\Alt^{s}Z

by
proposition

7.9
, since
s\dim Z<g

\displaystyle\;<\;(g-1)/2

\displaystyle\textnormal{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{Lemma:DecreasingDimensionInSmallDimension}, since $s<r$}.

In particular,
\dim Z<(g-1)/2
. But on the other hand

\displaystyle s\dim Z\;=\;(s+1)\dim Z-\dim Z

\displaystyle\;\geqslant\;g-\dim Z

since
(s+1)\dim Z\geqslant g

\displaystyle\;>\;(g+1)/2

since
\dim Z<(g-1)/2

which contradicts the previous displayed inequality.
∎

7.5.
Smooth wedge powers come from curves

It remains to show that the only smooth wedge powers in the dimension range in question are those coming from smooth curves, as announced in
theorem

7.6
.
In what follows, let
X,Z\subset A
be integral subvarieties such that

(1)

the subvariety
X\subset A
is smooth and nondivisible,

(2)

the Gauss maps
\gamma_{X}
and
\gamma_{Z}
are finite morphisms, and

(3)

we have
\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{[e](X)}
for some integers
e,r\geqslant 1
with
r\dim Z<\dim A
.

We claim that then
Y:=[e](X)
is birational to the
r
-th symmetric power of
Z
, more precisely:

Proposition 7.12
.

If the above conditions (1), (2), (3) hold, then
Y=Z+\cdots+Z
is a sum of
r
copies of
Z
, and the sum morphism
\sigma\colon Z^{r}\to Y
is finite and factors through a finite birational morphism

\tau\colon\hskip 10.00002pt\Sym^{r}Z\;=\;Z^{r}/\mathfrak{S}_{r}\;\longrightarrow\;Y.

Proof.

The finiteness of the Gauss map
\gamma_{Z}
implies that
Z\subset A
is nondegenerate by
theorem

2.8
. Since
r\dim Z<\dim A
,
proposition

7.9
says that
\pr_{Z,r}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}^{[r]}\to Z^{r}
is surjective and the sum morphism

\sigma\colon\hskip 10.00002ptZ^{r}\longrightarrow Y=Z+\cdots+Z

is generically finite. Moreover,
\sigma
induces a morphism
\tau\colon\Sym^{r}Z\to Y
and our assumption
\Alt^{r}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Y}
implies by part (2) of
proposition

7.9
that
\tau
has generic degree
\deg(\tau)=1
, i.e. it is birational. It remains to show that this morphism is finite. Since we have no control on the singularities of
Z
, we cannot use
proposition

B.4
. Instead, to show that
\sigma
and hence
\tau
is finite, we
consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}_{Z}^{[r]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{${\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{Z,r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{Z,r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{Y}}

Here, the fibers of the morphism
\pr_{Y}
are all of pure dimension
N=\codim_{A}Y-1
by
corollary

2.5
. Moreover, the morphism
\tilde{\sigma}
is finite since the rightmost square in the above diagram commutes and since in that square the horizontal arrows
\gamma_{Z,r}
and
\gamma_{Y}
are finite morphisms by our finiteness assumptions on Gauss maps. Hence, it follows that all fibers of
\pr_{Y}\circ\tilde{\sigma}\colon\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1984290}{447828}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1580154}{313481}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1345330}{222744}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathbb{V}$}}$}}}}}_{Z,r}\to Y
are of dimension
N
. Since
\sigma
is generically finite, it follows from the commutativity of the leftmost square in the above diagram that the generic fiber of the morphism
\pr_{Z,r}
has dimension
N
as well. We can now argue by contradiction: Any positive-dimensional fiber of
\sigma
would give rise to a fiber of
\pr_{Y}\circ\tilde{\sigma}
of dimension
\geqslant N+1
, by semi-continuity of dimension of fibers for proper morphisms
[
Sta22
,
lemma 0D4I
]
. This shows that
\sigma
is finite.
∎

Proof of
theorem

7.6
.

In
proposition

7.12
, we have seen that the sum morphism
\sigma
factors through a finite birational morphism
\tau\colon\Sym^{r}Z\to Y
.
We claim that a similar finite birational morphism from a symmetric product also exists for
X
rather than for
Y=[e](X)
. For this, we use the following general remark:

For any integral subvariety
W\subset A
, the scheme-theoretic preimage
[e]^{-1}(W)
is reduced because it is the preimage of a reduced subvariety under an étale morphism
[
Sta22
,
prop. 03PC (8)
]
. If
W^{\prime}
is an irreducible component of
[e]^{-1}(W)
, then any other irreducible component is of the form
W^{\prime}+x
for an
e
-torsion point
x\in A[e]
, and hence the morphism

[e]\colon W^{\prime}\to Z
is surjective. In particular, by
remark

2.6
the subvariety
W\subset A
is nondegenerate if and only if
W^{\prime}\subset A
is so.

Applying this to
W=Z
, we see that any irreducible component
Z^{\prime}
of
[e]^{-1}(Z)
is nondegenerate. Hence, if we define
X^{\prime}=Z^{\prime}+\cdots+Z^{\prime}\subset A
to be the sum of
r
copies of
Z^{\prime}
, then
lemma

2.7
implies

\dim X^{\prime}=r\dim Z^{\prime}=r\dim Z=\dim Y.

It follows that
X^{\prime}
is an irreducible component of
[e]^{-1}(Y)
. On the other hand, we have

[e]^{-1}(Y)\;=\;[e]^{-1}([e](X))\;=\;\bigcup_{t\in A[e]}X+t.

Therefore, there is an
e
-torsion point
t\in A[e]
such that
X=X^{\prime}+t
and
X
is the sum of
r
copies of

\tilde{Z}:=Z^{\prime}+u,

where
u\in A(k)
is any point with
ru=t
. Since the stabilizer of
X=\tilde{Z}+\cdots+\tilde{Z}
is trivial by assumption, it follows that the stabilizer of
\tilde{Z}
is trivial, so the finite morphism
[e]\colon\tilde{Z}\to Z
is birational. Then the morphism

\Sym^{r}[e]\colon\hskip 10.00002pt\Sym^{r}\tilde{Z}\;\longrightarrow\;\Sym^{r}Z

is finite birational by
proposition

B.2
. Now the sum morphism
\tilde{\sigma}\colon\tilde{Z}^{r}\to X
is invariant under the permutation action of
\mathfrak{S}_{r}
, so it factors through a morphism
\tilde{\tau}
as shown in the following commutative diagram:

(7.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{r}\tilde{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\ }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{r}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{r}[e]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[e]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

The morphisms
\tau
,
[e]
and
\Sym^{r}[e]
in this diagram are finite birational, hence
\tilde{\tau}
must be finite birational, too. On the other hand, the variety
X
is smooth by hypothesis, which forces
\tilde{\tau}
to be an isomorphism. In particular, the symmetric power
\Sym^{r}\tilde{Z}
is smooth, which for
r>1
implies that
\tilde{Z}
is a smooth curve by
proposition

B.3
.
∎

Remark 7.13
.

Given
X,Y,Z
as in the proof of theorem
7.6
, it follows immediately from the fact that the morphisms
[e]
and
\tau
from (
7.2
) are finite and birational that
X
is dominated by the normalization
W
of
\Sym^{r}Z
which in turn is easily seen to be isomorphic to
\Sym^{r}\tilde{Z}
where
\tilde{Z}
is the normalization of
Z
. Smoothness of
X
implies that in fact
\Sym^{r}\tilde{Z}\simeq X
. The argument we used instead is maybe a bit longer but yields more, namely a canonical (up to an
e
-torsion point) embedding of
\tilde{Z}
into
A
.

8.
Spin representations

We now show that under suitable assumptions on a smooth subvariety of an abelian variety, its Tannaka group cannot be the image of a spin group acting via a spin representation. For hypersurfaces this can be done by showing that their topological Euler characteristic is not a power of two
[
LS20
, lemma 4.9]
; we here discuss the case of higher codimension, where we do not know the Euler characteristic but consider characteristic cycles as in the previous section.

8.1.
Statement of the main result

Recall that for
N\geqslant 3
the group
\SO_{N}(\mathbb{F})
admits a double cover

\Spin_{N}(\mathbb{F})\;\longrightarrow\;\SO_{N}(\mathbb{F})

by the spin group. The spin group is a simply connected algebraic group and admits a faithful representation
\mathbb{S}_{N}\in\textup{Rep}_{\mathbb{F}}(\Spin_{N}(\mathbb{F}))
, the
spin representation
of dimension
\dim S_{N}=2^{n}
where
n=\lfloor N/2\rfloor
. The behavior of this representation depends on the Dynkin type (see
